When we finally camped and built our igloos, it was not with any degree of comfort that we lay down to rest. Actually it was more comfortable to keep on the march, and when we did rest it was fatigue that compelled.

===Chinese===
3月17日：除马文教授和他的分队之外，全队都留在营中。马文被派到前头，为大队接下来几个行程勘定路线；营中众人则忙于修理雪橇和装备的种种活计。

18日早晨，主队已整装待发，而且说走就走——尽管天气和冰况让人心里发沉。温度计零下40度，右侧和前方的松散浮冰分明在移动，所幸是朝北移去。一股烈风级的狂风从背后刮来；头三英里我们行进缓慢。冰丘乱得七零八落，翻越极为艰难，雪橇一再翻倒；有一架雪橇破损得厉害，只好停下修理。修橇的当口，我们吃了饼干，权当午间的便饭。狗则什么都没有喂——经验早已教会我们：狗怀着日后有赏的盼头干活，比赏已落肚的时候更卖力气。

那一整天，空气里雾霭冰霜浓得化不开，我们觉出的寒冷，甚至比气温更低却天空晴朗时还要厉害。风总能找到衣物上最细小的缝隙，像飞针一般扎进我们的身子。风帽同我们日渐长起的胡须冻在了一起，每到停下的时候，我们只得把那些冰敲碎剥掉——那是我们的呼气凝成、又混着身上蒸出的汗气冻结而成的。最后扎营筑起伊格鲁时，我们躺下歇息，谈不上有半分舒适。实际上，继续赶路反倒更舒服些；而我们真正停下来休息，是被疲惫逼出来的。

## XIII / 十三

===Original===
The supporting-parties begin to turn back.
March 19: We left camp in a haze of bitter cold; the ice conditions about the same as the previous day; high rafters, huge and jagged; and we pickaxed the way continuously. By noontime, we found ourselves alongside of a lead covered by a film of young ice. We forced the dogs and they took it on the run, the ice undulating beneath them, the same as it does when little wanton boys play at *tickley benders*, often with serious results, on the newly formed ice on ponds and brooks down in civilization. Our *tickley benders* were not done in the spirit of play, but on account of urgent necessity, and as it was I nearly suffered a serious loss of precious possessions.
One of the sledges, driven by Ahwatingwah, broke through the ice and its load, which consisted of my extra equipment, such as kamiks, mittens, etc., was thoroughly soaked. Luckily for the boy, he was at the side of the sledge and escaped a ducking. Foolishly I rushed over, but, quickly realizing my danger, I slowed down, and with the utmost care he fished out the sledge, and the dogs, shaking as with palsy, were gently urged on. Walking wide, like the polar bear, we crept after, and without further incident reached the opposite side of the lead. My team had reached there before me and, with human intelligence, the dogs had dragged the sledge to a place of safety and were sitting on their haunches, with ears cocked forward, watching us in our precarious predicament. They seemed to rejoice at our deliverance, and as I went among them and untangled their traces I could not forbear giving each one an affectionate pat on the head.
For the next five hours our trail lay over heavy pressure ridges, in some places sixty feet high. We had to make a trail over the mountains of ice and then come back for the sledges. A difficult climb began. Pushing from our very toes, straining every muscle, urging the dogs with voice and whip, we guided the sledges. On several occasions the dogs gave it up, standing still in their tracks, and we had to hold the sledges with the strength of our bones and muscles to prevent them from sliding backwards. When we had regained our equilibrium the dogs were again started, and in this way we gained the tops of the pressure-ridges.

===Chinese===
各支援队开始折返。
3月19日：我们在苦寒的一片迷蒙中拔营；冰情同前一天大致相仿：冰梁高耸，巨大而尖峭；我们一路抡镐，不停地开路。到正午时分，我们来到一条水道旁，水面上结着一层新冰的薄膜。我们硬驱狗群，它们飞奔着冲了过去，脚下的冰面随之起伏荡动，就像文明世界里顽劣的孩童在池塘溪流的新冰上玩*弯冰游戏*（tickley benders）时的光景——那种玩法，常常落得严重的后果。我们这番*弯冰游戏*可不是抱着玩耍的兴致，而是出于紧迫的必要；即便如此，我也险些蒙受一场损失，丢掉一批珍贵的物件。
阿瓦廷瓦（Ahwatingwah）驾驭的一架雪橇破冰落水，橇上载着我的备用装备——卡米克（kamiks，海豹皮靴）、连指手套之类——全都湿了个透。这孩子算他走运，人正在雪橇旁边，免了一场落水之灾。我一时莽撞，直冲了过去，但随即意识到自己身处险境，赶紧放慢了脚步；他以最大的谨慎把雪橇从水里捞了出来；那些狗抖得像中了麻痹症似的，被轻轻地驱赶着继续前进。我们学着北极熊的样子，大岔着步子，在后面缓缓挪动，没再出什么岔子，便到了水道的对岸。我的狗队比我先到，那些狗带着人一般的灵性，已把雪橇拖到安全的地方，正蹲坐在那里，双耳前竖，看着我们身陷险境的狼狈模样。它们似乎在为我们的脱险而欢喜；我走到它们中间，解开绞缠的挽绳时，忍不住在每一只的头上亲昵地拍了一拍。
接下来的五个钟头，我们的路都走在高大的冰脊上，有些地方高达六十英尺。我们不得不先在那些冰山般的脊岭上开出一条道，再返回去接雪橇。艰难的攀登开始了。我们从脚趾尖起使着劲，绷紧浑身每一块肌肉，用喊声和鞭子驱策狗群，引导着雪橇。有好几回狗干脆撂了挑子，立在原地一动不动，我们只得凭一身筋骨之力死死拽住雪橇，不让它向后滑坠。待重新稳住平衡，再把狗赶起来，就这样，我们一程程登上了冰脊的顶。

===Original===
Going down on the opposite side was more nerve-racking. On the descent of one ridge, in spite of the experienced care of Ootah, the sledge bounded away from him, and at a declivity of thirty feet was completely wrecked. The frightened dogs dashed wildly in every direction to escape the falling sledge, and as quickly as possible we slid down the steep incline, at the same time guiding the dogs attached to the two remaining sledges. We rushed over, my two boys and I, to the spot where the poor dogs stood trembling with fright. We released them from the tangle they were in, and, with kind words and pats of the hand on their heads, quieted them. For over an hour we struggled with the broken pieces of the wreck and finally lashed them together with strips of *oog-sook* (seal-hide). We said nothing to the Commander when he caught up with us, but his quick eye took in at a glance the experience we had been through. The repairs having been completed, we again started. Before us stretched a heavy, old floe, giving us good going until we reached the lead, when the order was given to camp. We built our igloos, and boiled the tea and had what we called supper.
Commander Peary called me over to his igloo and gave me my orders: first; that I should at once select the best dogs of the three teams, as the ones disqualified by me would on the following morning be sent back to the ship, in care of the third supporting party, which was to turn back. Secondly; that I should rearrange the loads on the remainder of the sledges, there now being ten in number. It was eight p.m. when I began work and two the following morning when I had finished.
March 20: During the night, the Commander had a long talk with Borup, and in the morning my good friend, in command of the third supporting party, bade us all goodbye and took his detachment back to land and headquarters. There were three Eskimos and seventeen dogs in his party. A fine and plucky young man, whose cheerful manner and ready willingness had made him a prime favorite; and he had done his work like an old campaigner.
At the time of Borup’s turning southward, Captain Bartlett, with two Eskimos, started out to the north to make trail. He was to act as pioneer. At ten-thirty a.m., I, with two Eskimos, followed; leaving at the igloos the Commander and Professor Marvin, with four Eskimos. The system of our marches from now on was that the first party, or pioneers, which consisted of Captain Bartlett, myself, and our Eskimos, should be trail-making, while the second party, consisting of Commander Peary and Marvin, with their Eskimos, should be sleeping; and while the first party was sleeping, the second should be traveling over the trail previously made. The sun was above the horizon the whole twenty-four hours of the day, and accordingly there was no darkness. Either the first or second party was always traveling, and progress was hourly made.

===Chinese===
从另一侧下去，更叫人心惊胆战。下的一道冰脊时，尽管乌塔经验老到、处处留心，雪橇还是从他手里脱了出去，在一处三十英尺的陡坡下摔了个彻底散架。受惊的狗为了躲开翻滚而下的雪橇，向四面八方狂窜；我们尽快顺着陡坡滑下去，一面还要驾驭另外两架雪橇上的狗。我和我的两个小伙子赶忙冲到那可怜的狗群惊得发抖的地方，把它们从缠绞中解脱出来，用好言好语和抚头的轻拍让它们安定下来。有一个多钟头，我们同残骸的碎块搏斗，最后用*乌格苏克*（oog-sook，海豹皮）裁成的条子把碎块捆扎到一起。指挥官赶上来时，我们对这事只字未提，可他那双锐利的眼睛一眼就看透了我们方才的经历。修葺完毕，我们重新上路。眼前铺展着一片厚重的大浮冰，走起来十分顺当，一直走到水道边上，扎营的命令才下来。我们垒起伊格鲁，煮好了茶，吃了顿我们所谓的晚饭。
皮尔里指挥官把我叫到他的伊格鲁里，向我交代命令：第一，我应立即从三支狗队里挑出最好的狗，因为经我裁定不合格的那些，次日清晨要交由即将折返的第三支援队送回船上；第二，我应重新安排余下雪橇的载重——如今一共是十架。我动手干活时是晚上八点，干完时已是第二天凌晨两点。
3月20日：夜里，指挥官同博鲁普作了一次长谈；早晨，我这位好朋友——第三支援队的队长——同我们大家一一作别，带着他的分队回陆地上的总部去了。他那一队有三名因纽特人和十七条狗。一个优秀而有胆气的年轻人，他那乐天的作风和随叫随到的热忱，使他处处招人喜爱；而他干起工作来，又像一位沙场老手。
就在博鲁普折向南去的时候，巴特利特船长带着两名因纽特人向北进发，去开辟路径。他充当开路先锋。上午十点半，我带着两名因纽特人尾随而上；伊格鲁里留下指挥官和马文教授，带着四名因纽特人。从今往后的行进章法是：第一队，即先锋队，由巴特利特船长、我和我们的因纽特人组成，负责开路；第二队由皮尔里指挥官、马文和他们的因纽特人组成，趁第一队开路时睡觉；等第一队睡下，第二队便沿着先前开出的路径行进。太阳全天二十四小时都悬在地平线以上，因此没有黑暗。第一队或第二队总有一队在赶路，时时刻刻都在前进。

===Original===
March 21: Captain Bartlett got away early, leaving me in camp to await the arrival of Commander Peary and Marvin, with their party; and it was eight a.m. when they arrived. Commander Peary instructed me to the effect that, when I overtook the Captain, I should tell him to make as much speed as possible.
The going was, for the first hour, over rough, raftered ice. Great care and caution had to be observed, but after that we reached a stretch of undulated, level ice, extending easily fifteen miles; and the exhilarating effect made our spirits rise. The snow-covering was soft, but with the help of our snowshoes we paced off the miles, and at noon we caught up with the Captain and his boys. Together we traveled on, and at the end of an hour’s going we halted for our noon-meal, consisting of a can of tea and three biscuits per man, the dogs doing the hungry looking on, as dogs have done and do and will do forever. As we sat and ate, we joshed each other, and the Eskimo boys joined in the good-natured raillery.
The meal did not detain us long, and soon we were pushing on again as quickly as possible over the level ice, fearing that if we delayed the condition of the ice would change, for changes come suddenly, and frequently without warning. At nine p.m. we camped, the Captain having been on the go for fifteen hours, and I for thirteen; and we estimated that we had a good fourteen miles to our credit.
March 22 was the finest day we had, and it was a day of unusual clearness and calm; practically no wind and a cloudless sky. The fields of ice and snow sparkled and glistened and the daylight lasted for the full twenty-four hours. It was six a.m. when Egingwah, the Commander’s Eskimo courier, reached our camp, with the note of command and encouragement; and immediately the Captain and I left camp.

===Chinese===
3月21日：巴特利特船长一早动身了，留我在营中等候皮尔里指挥官和马文率队到来；他们抵达时已是上午八点。皮尔里指挥官向我交代说，等我追上船长，就叫他尽可能加快速度。
头一个钟头的路，走在崎岖的叠压冰上，必须格外小心在意；过了那一段，我们来到一片微微起伏的平整冰面，绵延足有十五英里；那令人振奋的情景使我们精神大振。雪盖松软，但借着雪鞋，我们一英里一英里地量了过去，正午时分赶上了船长和他的小伙子们。我们结伴继续行进，走满一个钟头后停下吃午饭：每人一罐茶、三块饼干；狗在一旁饿眼巴巴地望着，正如狗过去如此、现在如此、将来也永远如此。我们坐着进餐时彼此打趣逗乐，因纽特小伙子们也加入了这场善意的取笑。
这顿饭没耽搁我们多久，很快我们又尽快地在平整的冰面上赶路，唯恐稍有迟延冰情就会改变，因为变化来得突然，而且往往毫无征兆。晚上九点我们扎营，船长已连走十五个钟头，我走了十三个；我们估计，足足有十四英里记在了我们的账上。
3月22日是我们一路上最晴美的一天，天色异常清澈宁静；几乎无风，天空万里无云。冰雪的田野闪闪发亮，白昼持续了整整二十四个钟头。早晨六点，指挥官的因纽特信使埃金瓦来到我们营地，带来一道号令与鼓励的便条；船长和我随即启程离开营地。

===Original===
Stretching to the northward was a brilliantly illuminated, level, and slightly drifted snow-plain, our imperial highway, presenting a spectacle grand and sublime; and we were truly grateful and inwardly prayed that this condition would last indefinitely. Without incident or accident, we marched on for fifteen hours, pacing off mile after mile in our steady northing, and at nine p.m. we halted. It was then we realized how utterly fatigued and exhausted we were. It took us over an hour and a half to build our igloos. We had a hard time finding suitable snow conditions for building them, and the weather was frightfully cold. The evening meal of pemmican-stew and tea was prepared, the dogs were fed, and we turned in.
March 23: Our sleep-banked eyes were opened by the excitement caused by the arrival of Marvin and his division. He reported the same good going that we had had the day before, and also that he had taken an elevation of the sun and computed his latitude as 85° 46′ north. We turned the igloos over to Marvin and his Eskimos, who were to await the arrival of the Commander, and Captain Bartlett and myself got our parties under way.
Conditions are never similar, no two days are the same; and our going this day was nothing like the paradise of the day before. At a little distance from the igloos we encountered high masses of heavily-rubbled, old ice. The making of a trail through these masses of ice caused us to use our pickaxes continuously. It was backing and filling all of the time. First we would reconnoiter, then we would hew our way and make the trail, then we would go back and, getting in the traces, help the dogs pull the sledges, which were still heavily loaded. This operation was repeated practically all the day of March 23, except for the last hour of traveling, when we zigzagged to the eastward, where the ice appeared less formidable, consisting of small floes with rubble ice between and a heavy, old floe beyond. There we camped. The latitude was 85° 46′ north.
The course from the land to the Pole was not direct and due north, for we followed the lines of least resistance, and frequently found ourselves going due east or west, in order to detour around pressure ridges, floebergs, and leads.

===Chinese===
向北铺展着一片被照得通明的、平整而微有积雪堆积的雪原——我们的帝王大道——呈现出宏大壮丽的景象；我们由衷感激，内心默默祈祷这般境况能够长久保持。我们平安无事地行进了十五个钟头，在平稳的北进中一英里接一英里地量过去，晚上九点才停下。直到那时，我们才意识到自己是何等地疲乏劳顿。我们花了一个半钟头才垒起伊格鲁。要找到适于筑屋的雪况很费了一番周折，天气又冷得骇人。干肉饼炖菜加茶的晚饭做好，喂罢狗群，我们便就寝了。
3月23日：马文和他那一队人马到来引起的一阵骚动，把我们睡意沉沉的眼睛睁了开来。他报告说，路况同我们前一天走过的一样好，还说他观测了太阳高度，算出纬度为北纬85度46分。我们把伊格鲁移交给马文和他的因纽特人，由他们等候指挥官到来；巴特利特船长和我则带着各自的人马上路。
境况从不雷同，没有哪两天是一样的；我们这一天的行路，同前一天那种乐园般的境况毫无相似之处。离伊格鲁不远，我们就遇上了大片高耸、碎冰垒积的老冰。要在这些冰块中间开路，我们只得不停地抡冰镐。一整天都是进进退退：先侦察一番，再斩出通路、开出道来，然后折回去，套进挽绳，帮着狗拉那些依旧满载的雪橇。这套程序在3月23日几乎重演了一整天，只有最后一小时的行进除外——那一段我们之字形地向东行进，那边的冰看来不那么骇人：由一块块小浮冰组成，浮冰之间夹着碎冰，再往外是一片厚重的大浮冰。我们就在那里扎营。纬度是北纬85度46分。
从陆地到北极点的路线并非笔直向正北，因为我们走的是阻力最小的路线，常常发觉自己在向正东或正西行进，为的是绕开冰脊、浮冰山和水道。

===Original===
March 24: Commander Peary reached camp shortly after six a.m., and after a few brief instructions, we started out. The going not as heavy as the day previous; but the sky overcast, and a heavy drift on the surface made it decidedly unpleasant for the dogs. For the first six hours the going was over rough, jagged ice, covered with deep, soft snow; for the rest of the day it improved. We encountered comparatively level ice, with a few hummocks, and in places covered with deep snow. We camped at eight p.m., beside a very heavy pressure-ridge as long as a city street and as high as the houses along the street.
March 25: Turned out at four-thirty a.m., to find a steadily falling snow storm upon us. We breakfasted, and fifteen minutes later we were once more at work making trail. Our burly neighbor, the pressure-ridge, in whose lee we had spent the night, did not make an insuperable obstacle, and in the course of an hour we had made a trail across it, and returned to the igloo for the sledges. We found that the main column had reached camp, and after greetings had been given, Commander Peary called me aside and gave me my orders; to take the trail at once, to speed it up to the best of my ability and cover as much distance as possible; for he intended that I should remain at the igloo the following day to sort out the best dogs and rearrange the loads, as Marvin was to turn back with the fourth supporting-party. My heart stopped palpitating, I breathed easier, and my mind was relieved. It was not my turn yet, I was to continue onward and there only remained one person between me and the Pole﻿—the Captain. We knew Commander Peary’s general plan: that, at the end of certain periods, certain parties would turn south to the land and the ship; but we did not know who would comprise or command those parties and, until I had the Commander’s word, I feared that I would be the next after Borup. At the same time, I did not see how Marvin could travel much longer, as his feet were very badly frozen.
Obedient to the Commander’s orders, the Captain, I, and our Eskimos, left camp with loaded sledges and trudged over the newly made trail, coming to rough ice which stretched for a distance of five miles, and kept us hard at back-straining, shoulder-wrenching work for several hours. The rest of the day’s march was over level, unbroken, young ice; and the distance covered was considerable.

===Chinese===
3月24日：早晨六点刚过，皮尔里指挥官到达营地，简短交代了几句，我们便出发了。路况不似前一天那般沉重；但天空阴沉，冰面上浓重的吹雪让狗走得很不痛快。头六个钟头走的是崎岖尖利的冰面，覆着又深又软的积雪；当天余下的时间里，路况有所好转。我们遇上的是比较平整的冰面，只有几处冰丘，有些地方盖着厚雪。晚上八点我们扎营，营地旁是一道极其厚重的冰脊，有一条城市街道那么长，同街边的房屋一样高。
3月25日：早晨四点半起身，发现一场不紧不慢、下个不停的风雪正压在我们头上。我们吃了早饭，一刻钟之后便又干起开路的活计。我们那魁伟的邻居——那道冰脊，我们就在它的背风处过的夜——并未构成不可逾越的障碍，不出一个钟头，我们就在它身上开出了一条道，随后折回伊格鲁去取雪橇。我们发现主力纵队已经抵达营地，寒暄已毕，皮尔里指挥官把我叫到一旁，向我交代命令：立即上路，竭尽所能加快速度，赶出尽可能多的路程；因为他打算第二天让我留在伊格鲁，挑选最好的狗、重新安排载重，因为马文将随第四支援队折返。我的心不再突突乱跳，呼吸顺畅了些，心里也松快了。还没轮到我，我还要继续向前，在我和北极点之间，只剩下一个人——船长。我们都知道皮尔里指挥官的总体计划：每过一定的时期，便有一定的队伍折向南方的陆地和船上去；但我们并不知道那些队伍由哪些人组成、归谁统领；在没有得到指挥官亲口的话之前，我一直担心自己会是博鲁普之后的下一个。与此同时，我也看不出马文还能怎样走下去，因为他的双脚冻得非常厉害。
我们遵照指挥官的命令，船长、我和我们的因纽特人带着载重的雪橇离开营地，沿着新开出的路径跋涉，来到一段绵延五英里的崎岖冰面，压得我们弯腰拽肩、苦干了好几个钟头。这一天余下的行程走在平整、连片的新冰上；走过的路程相当可观。

===Original===
March 26: The Commander and party reached the igloo at ten-forty-five a.m. Captain Bartlett had taken to the trail at six a.m., and was now miles to the northward, out of sight. I immediately started to work on the task assigned me by the Commander, assorting the dogs first, so that the different king dogs could fight it out and adjust themselves to new conditions while I was rearranging the loads.
At twelve, noon, Professor Marvin took his final sight, and after figuring it out told me that he made it 86° 38′ north.
The work of readjusting the loads kept me busy until seven p.m. While doing this work I came across my Bible that I had neglected so long, and that night, before going to sleep, I read the twenty-third Psalm, and the fifth chapter of St. Matthew.
March 27: I was to take the trail at six a.m., but before starting I went over to Marvin’s igloo to bid him goodbye. In his quiet, earnest manner, he advised me to keep on, and hoped for our success; he congratulated me and we gave each other the strong, fraternal grip of our honored fraternity and we confidently expected to see each other again at the ship. My good, kind friend was never again to see us, or talk with us. It is sad to write this. He went back to his death, drowned in the cold, black water of the Big Lead. In unmarked, unmarbled grave, he sleeps his last, long sleep.

===Chinese===
3月26日：上午十点三刻，指挥官一行到达伊格鲁。巴特利特船长早晨六点已经上路，此刻已在数英里之外的北方，望不见了。我随即着手办理指挥官派给我的差事，先分拣狗群，好让各队的狗王趁我重新安排载重的当口打出个高下、各自适应新的局面。
正午十二点，马文教授进行了他最后一次观测，计算完毕后告诉我，测得的是北纬86度38分。
重新调整载重的活计一直让我忙到晚上七点。干这活的时候，我翻出了那本被我冷落已久的《圣经》，那天夜里就寝之前，我读了《诗篇》第二十三篇和《马太福音》第五章。
3月27日：我定于早晨六点上路，但出发之前，先到马文的伊格鲁去同他道别。他以他那安静而恳切的口吻勉励我坚持下去，祝愿我们成功；他向我道贺，我们彼此紧握了手——那是我们那个受人敬重的兄弟会有力的兄弟之握——我们都满心笃定，指望回到船上再相见。我这位善良仁厚的朋友，从此再没能见到我们，再没能同我们说话。写下这一笔，令人心酸。他走向了自己的死亡：溺没在「大水道」冰冷乌黑的水中。在一座没有标记、没有大理石墓碑的坟茔里，他睡着他最后的长眠。

## XIV / 十四

===Original===
Bartlett’s farthest north﻿—His quiet goodbye.

Leaving the Commander and Marvin at the igloos, my party took up the Captain’s trail northward. It was expected that Peary would follow in an hour and that at the same time Marvin would start his return march. After a few minutes’ going, we came to young ice of this season, broken up and frozen solid, not difficult to negotiate, but requiring constant pulling; leaving this, we came to an open lead which caused us to make a detour to the westward for four miles. We crossed on ice so thin that one of the sledge-runners broke through, and a little beyond one of the dogs fell in so completely that it was a precarious effort to rescue him; but we made it and, doglike, he shook the water out of his fur and a little later, when his fur froze, I gave him a thorough beating; not for falling in the water, but in order to loosen the ice-particles, so that he could shake them off. Poor brute, it was no use, and in a short while he commenced to develop symptoms of the dread piblokto, so in mercy he was killed. One of the Eskimo boys did the killing.

Dangerous as the crossing was, it was the only place possible, and we succeeded far better than we had anticipated. Beyond the lead we came to an old floe and, beyond that, young ice of one season’s formation, similar to that which had been encountered earlier in the day. Before us lay a heavy, old floe, covered with soft, deep snow in which we sank continually; but it was only five p.m. when we reached the Captain’s igloo. Anticipating the arrival of the Commander, we built another igloo, and about an hour and a half later the Commander and his party came in.

===Chinese===
巴特利特的最北点——他平静的告别。

把指挥官和马文留在伊格鲁，我这一队人循着船长的踪迹向北进发。按预计，皮尔里会在一个钟头后跟上来，与此同时马文也将启程踏上归途。走了几分钟，我们便上了本季的新冰——冰已破碎又重新冻实，通行并不难，只是需要不停地拉拽；离开这片冰，我们遇上一条未封冻的水道，只得向西绕行了四英里。渡河踩的冰薄得很，一根雪橇滑木都压破了冰面；再往前一点，一只狗整个儿陷了进去，把它救上来真是一场险之又险的营救；不过我们成功了，它也真不愧是条狗，抖净了毛上的水；过了一阵，等毛上的水冻住，我把它结结实实揍了一顿——不是因为它落了水，而是为了震松粘在毛上的冰粒，好让它抖落干净。可怜的畜生，这一切毫无用处，没多久它便开始显出那种可怕的皮布洛克托（piblokto）的症状，出于怜悯，只好把它杀了。动手的是一名因纽特小伙子。

这次渡河虽然凶险，却是唯一可行的去处，而我们渡得远比预想的顺利。过了水道，我们来到一片老浮冰上，再往前，是一季之内生成的新冰，同当天早些时候遇到的那片相仿。我们面前横着一片厚重的大浮冰，覆着又软又深的积雪，我们一步一陷；但到达船长的伊格鲁时，才不过下午五点。料着指挥官即将到来，我们又垒起一座伊格鲁，约莫一个半钟头后，指挥官和他的一行人到了。

===Original===
March 28: Exactly 40° below zero when we pushed the sledges up to the curled-up dogs and started them off over rough ice covered with deep soft snow. It was like walking in loose granulated sugar. Indeed I might compare the snow of the Arctic to the granules of sugar, without their saccharine sweetness, but with freezing cold instead; you can not make snowballs of it, for it is too thoroughly congealed, and when it is packed by the wind it is almost as solid as ice. It is from the packed snow that the blocks used to form the igloo-walls are cut.

At the end of four hours, we came to the igloo where the Captain and his boys were sleeping the sleep of utter exhaustion. In order not to interrupt the Captain’s rest, we built another igloo and unloaded his sledge, and distributed the greater part of the load among the sledges of the party. The Captain, on awakening, told us that the journey we had completed on that day had been made by him under the most trying conditions, and that it had taken him fourteen hours to do it. We were able to make better time because we had his trail to follow, and, therefore, the necessity of finding the easiest way was avoided. That was the object of the scout or pioneer party and Captain Bartlett had done practically all of it up to the time he turned back at 87° 48′ north.

March 29: You have undoubtedly taken into consideration the pangs of hunger and of cold that you know assailed us, going Poleward; but have you ever considered that we were thirsty for water to drink or hungry for fat? To eat snow to quench our thirsts would have been the height of folly, and as well as being thirsty, we were continuously assailed by the pangs of a hunger that called for the fat, good, rich, oily, juicy fat that our systems craved and demanded.

===Chinese===
3月28日：我们把雪橇推到蜷作一团的狗跟前时，温度恰好零下40度；随后赶着它们出发，走上覆盖着深厚软雪的崎岖冰面。那就像走在散粒的砂糖里。的确，我不妨把北极的雪比作砂糖的颗粒，只是没有那份糖的甜，取而代之的是砭人肌骨的严寒；这种雪团不成雪球，因为它冻得太过彻底，而一经风压实，便几乎同冰一样坚硬。砌伊格鲁墙用的雪块，正是从这种压实的雪中切出来的。

走满四个钟头，我们来到一座伊格鲁，船长和他的小伙子们正在里面酣睡，那是筋疲力尽之后的酣睡。为了不打搅船长休息，我们另垒了一座伊格鲁，卸下他的雪橇，把大部分载重分摊到全队的雪橇上。船长醒来后对我们说，我们当天走完的这段路，他当初是在最艰苦的条件下闯出来的，整整花了他十四个钟头。我们之所以能走得更快，是因为有他开出的路径可循，从而免去了寻觅最省力走法的必要。这正是侦察队或者说先锋队的职责所在；而在他于北纬87度48分折返之前，这份差事几乎全由巴特利特船长一人包办了。

3月29日：诸位想必早已思量过，我们在北去的路上遭受着怎样的饥火与寒苦的煎熬；可你们可曾想过，我们还渴着想喝的水、馋着想吃的脂肪？靠吃雪解渴，无异于愚蠢透顶；何况在干渴之外，我们还时时刻刻受着另一种饥饿的煎熬——那饥饿呼唤的是脂肪：上好的、丰腴的、油润多汁的脂肪，我们的身体渴求着它、索要着它。

===Original===
Had we succumbed to the temptations of thirst and eaten the snow, we would not be able to tell the tale of the conquest of the Pole; for the result of eating snow is death. True, the dogs licked up enough moisture to quench their thirsts, but we were not made of such stern stuff as they. Snow would have reduced our temperatures and we would quickly have fallen by the way. We had to wait until camp was made and the fire of alcohol started before we had a chance, and it was with hot tea that we quenched our thirsts. The hunger for fat was not appeased; a dog or two was killed, but his carcass went to the Eskimos and the entrails were fed to the rest of the pack. We ate no dogs on this trip, for various reasons, mainly, that the eating of dog is only a last resort, and we had plenty of food, and raw dog is flavorless and very tough. The killing of a dog is such a horrible matter that I will not describe it, and it is permitted only when all other exigencies have been exhausted. An Eskimo does not permit one drop of blood to escape.

The morning of the 29th of March, 1909, a heavy and dense fog of frost spicules overhung the camp. At four a.m., the Captain left camp to make as far a northing as possible. I with my Eskimos followed later. On our way we passed over very rough ice alternating with small floes, young ice of a few months’ duration, and one old floe. We were now beside a lead of over three hundred feet in width, which we were unable to cross at that time because the ice was running steadily, though to the Northward. Following the trail of the Captain, which carried us a little to the westward of the lead, within one hundred feet of the Captain’s igloo, the order to camp was given, as going forward was impossible. The whole party was together farther north than had ever been made by any other human beings, and in perfectly good condition; but the time was quickly coming when the little party would have to be made smaller and some part of it sent back. We were too fatigued to argue the question.

We turned in for a rest and sleep, but soon turned out again in pandemonium incomprehensible; the ice moving in all directions, our igloos wrecked, and every instant our very lives in danger. With eyes dazed by sleep, we tried to guide the terror-stricken dogs and push the sledges to safety, but rapidly we saw the party being separated and the black water begin to appear amid the roar of the breaking ice floes.

===Chinese===
倘若我们屈服于干渴的诱惑，吃了雪，今天便无法向人讲述征服北极点的故事；因为吃雪的下场就是死亡。诚然，狗舔食一点冰雪便足以解渴，可我们并不是它们那副刚硬筋骨铸成的。雪会降低我们的体温，我们会很快倒毙在半道上。我们只能等到扎好营、点起酒精的炉火，才有解渴的机会，而真正给我们解渴的，是热茶。对脂肪的饥饿却得不到满足；宰了一两只狗，但胴体归了因纽特人，内脏喂了其余的狗。这次旅行我们没有吃狗，原因种种，主要的是：吃狗本是万不得已的最后手段，何况我们粮食充裕，而且生狗肉淡而无味、又老又韧。杀狗这件事太过骇人，我不愿细写；只有到了其他一切办法都用尽的地步，才容许这么做。因纽特人不肯让一滴血白白流失。

1909年3月29日早晨，一场由霜针凝成的浓重大雾笼罩着营地。凌晨四点，船长离营出发，以求尽量多向北赶一程。我和我的因纽特人随后跟上。一路上，我们走过极其崎岖的冰面，中间交替夹着小块浮冰、几个月冰龄的新冰，还有一片老浮冰。这时我们已来到一条三百多英尺宽的水道旁，当时无法渡越，因为道中的冰在不住地漂流，尽管是朝北漂去。我们循着船长的踪迹走，那踪迹把我们带到水道略偏西的一侧，在离船长的伊格鲁不到一百英尺的地方，扎营的命令下来了，因为再也无法前进。全队人马会聚一处，所在的纬度比古往今来任何人到过的都更靠北，而且个个状态完好；但是，这支小小队伍势必要再缩减、其中一部分人势必要被送回的时刻，很快就要到了。我们已疲乏得无心计较这个问题。

我们躺下歇息入睡，但很快又在一阵难以名状的大混乱中爬了起来：冰朝四面八方移动，我们的伊格鲁坍塌倾覆，性命时时刻刻危在旦夕。我们睁着睡意惺忪的眼睛，设法驾驭惊骇欲绝的狗群，把雪橇推向安全地带；可是眼看着队伍迅速被分隔开，黑色的海水在浮冰崩裂的轰鸣声中渐渐显露出来。

===Original===
To the westward of our igloo stood the Captain’s igloo, on an island of ice, which revolved, while swiftly drifting to the eastward. On one occasion the floe happened to strike the main floe. The Captain, intently watching his opportunity, quickly crossed with his Eskimos. He had scarcely set foot on the opposite floe when the floe on which he had been previously isolated swung off, and rapidly disappeared.

Once more the parties were together. Thoroughly exhausted, we turned in and fell asleep, myself and the Eskimos too dumb for utterance, and Commander Peary and Bartlett too full of the realization of our escape to have much to say.

The dogs were in very good condition, taking everything into consideration.

When we woke up it was the morning of another day, March 30, and we found open water all about us. We could not go on until either the lead had frozen or until it had raftered shut. Temperature 35° below zero, and the weather clear and calm with no visible motion of the ice. We spent the day industriously in camp, mending footgear, harness, clothing, and looking after the dogs and their traces. This was work enough, especially untangling the traces of the bewildered dogs. The traces, snarled and entangled, besides being frozen to the consistency of wire, gave us the hardest work; and, owing to the activity of the dogs in leaping and bounding over each other, we had the most *unideal* conditions possible to contend with, and we were handicapped by having to use mitted instead of ungloved fingers to untangle the snarls of knots. Unlike Alexander the Great, we dared not cut the “Gordian Knots,” but we did get them untangled.

===Chinese===
在我们伊格鲁的西面，立着船长的伊格鲁，坐落在一片冰岛上；那冰岛一边打着旋，一边急速向东漂去。有一回，那浮冰恰好撞上了主冰体。船长凝神盯住时机，带着他的因纽特人迅速冲越了过去。他前脚刚踏上对面的浮冰，先前困着他孤身其上的那块浮冰便荡了开去，倏忽消失得无影无踪。

几支队伍重新会合到了一起。我们彻底累垮了，躺下便沉沉睡去；我和因纽特人们哑然说不出话来，皮尔里指挥官和巴特利特则满心都是劫后余生的感慨，也说不上几句话。

把方方面面都算进去，狗群的状况还是非常之好。

等我们醒来，已是另一天的早晨，3月30日，我们发现四周全是未冻的明水。要么等水道冻住，要么等它撞挤封死，否则我们无法前进。气温零下35度，天气晴朗无风，冰面看不出一丝动静。我们在营中勤勤恳恳地忙了一整天，修补鞋靴、挽具和衣裳，照料狗群和它们的挽绳。这活计着实够干的了，尤其是解开那些晕头转向的狗身上绞成一团的挽绳。那些挽绳纠缠绞结，又冻得硬如铁丝，给我们添的麻烦最大；偏生狗们活蹦乱跳、彼此腾跃践踏，使我们陷入了最*不理想*的境地；加上我们只能戴着连指手套、不能用裸露的手指去解那一个个乱结，更是处处掣肘。我们不比亚历山大大帝，不敢挥剑斩断这“戈耳狄俄斯之结”，可结终究还是让我们一个个解开了。

===Original===
About five o’clock in the afternoon, the temperature had fallen to 43° below zero, and at the same time the ice began to move again. Owing to the attraction of the moon, the mighty flanks of the earth were being drawn by her invisible force, and were commencing again to crack and be rent asunder.

We loaded up hurriedly and all three parties left the camp and crossed over the place where recently had been the open lead, and beyond for more than five miles, until we reached the heavier and solid ice of the large floes. Northward our way led, and we kept on in that direction accordingly, at times crossing young ice so thin that the motion of the sledges would cause the ice to undulate. Over old floes of the blue, hummocky kind, on which the snow had fallen and become packed solid, the rest of this day’s journey was completed. We staggered into camp like drunken men, and built our igloos by force of habit rather than with the intelligence of human beings.

It was continuously daylight, but such a light as never was on land or sea.

The next day was April 1, and the Farthest North of Bartlett. I knew at this time that he was to go back, and that I was to continue, so I had no misgivings and neither had he. He was ready and anxious to take the back-trail. His five marches were up and he was glad of it, and he was told that in the morning he must turn back and knit the trail together, so that the main column could return over a beaten path.

===Chinese===
下午五点钟左右，气温已降到零下43度，与此同时，冰又动了起来。由于月亮的吸引，地球那广袤的侧翼正被她无形之力牵引着，又开始迸裂、撕作两半。

我们匆忙装橇，三支队伍一同离开营地，越过方才还是明水水道的地方，又继续向前走了五英里多，直到踏上大浮冰那更厚重坚实的冰面。我们的路指向北方，我们便照着这个方向走下去，有几次跨过的新冰薄得雪橇一过，冰面便随之起伏荡动。这一天余下的路程，都走在发蓝的、丘冈累累的老浮冰上，落下的积雪已压得坚实。我们像醉汉一样踉踉跄跄地进了营地，垒起伊格鲁——与其说是凭人的理智，毋宁说是出于习惯的驱使。

天光昼夜不熄，可那样的光，是陆上和海上从来不曾有过的。

第二天是4月1日，是巴特利特抵达最北点的一天。这时我知道，他就要回去了，而我将继续前进，所以我心里毫无忐忑，他也没有。他已准备停当，一心要走回头路。他的五段行程已经走满，他为此感到高兴；他得到通知：明晨必须折返，把一路的路径衔接起来，好让主力纵队沿着踩实的熟路回撤。

===Original===
Before going to sleep, Peary and he (Captain Bartlett) had figured out the reckoning of the distance, and, to insure the Captain’s making at least 88° north, Peary let him have another go for a short distance northward, and at noon on the day of his return, the observations showed that Captain Bartlett had made 87° 47′ North Latitude, or practically 88° north. “Why, Peary,” he said, “it is just like every day,” and so it was, with this exception, like every day in the Arctic, but with all of every day’s chances and hazards. The lion-like month of March had passed. Captain Bartlett bade us all farewell. He turned back from the Farthest North that had ever been reached by anyone, to insure the safe return of him who was to go to a still Farther North, the very top of the world, the Pole itself.

While waiting for Bartlett to return from his forced march, the main party had been at work, assorting dogs (by this time without much trouble, as only one was found utterly unfit to make progress), and rearranging loads, for the Captain had almost three hundred miles of sea-ice to negotiate before he would reach terra firma, and he had to have his food-supply arranged so that it would carry him to the land and back to the ship, and dogs in good enough condition to pull the loads, as well as enough sledges to bear his equipment. When he did come back to our camp, before the parting, he was perfectly satisfied, and with the same old confidence he swept his little party together and at three p.m., with a cheery “Goodbye! Good Luck!” he was off. His Eskimo boys, attempting in English, too, gave us their “Goodbyes.” The least emotional of all of our partings; and this brave man, who had borne the brunt of all of the hardships, like the true-blue, dead-game, unconquerable hero that he was, set out to do the work that was left for him to do; to knit the broken strands of our upward trail together, so that we who were at his rear could follow in safety.

I have never heard the story of the return of Captain Bartlett in detail; his Eskimo boys were incapable of telling it, and Captain Bartlett is altogether too modest.

===Chinese===
就寝之前，皮尔里和他（巴特利特船长）已经把航程推算清楚；为了确保船长至少到达北纬88度，皮尔里放他又向北赶了一小段。在他折返的那天正午，观测结果表明，巴特利特船长到达的是北纬87度47分，实际上等于北纬88度。“哎呀，皮尔里，”他说，“这一天同哪一天都一个样。”也确实如此——只此一桩例外——同北极里的每一天都一个样，只是把每一天全部的机缘与凶险都占全了。狮子般的3月过去了。巴特利特船长向我们大家道了别。他从人类所曾到达的最北点折返，为的是确保那个还要去往更北之地——世界的绝顶、北极点本身——的人能够平安归来。

等待巴特利特结束强行军归来的当口，主力队伍一直在忙活：分拣狗群（这时已没有多少麻烦，因为只找出一只实在走不动了的），重新安排载重。因为船长还要闯过将近三百英里的海冰，方能抵达坚实的陆地，他的口粮必须布置得够他走到陆地、再返回船上，狗的状态必须好到拉得动这些载重，还得有足够的雪橇装载他的行装。等他回到我们营地，临别之际，他心满意足，又怀着往日那股自信，把他那一小队人马拢到一处，下午三点，道一声爽朗的“再见！祝好运！”便动身去了。他的因纽特小伙子们也试着用英语，向我们道了声“再见”。这是我们历次话别中最不伤感的一次。这位勇士，一人当尽了千难万苦的头阵，以他那份忠贞不渝、宁折不弯、不可战胜的英雄本色，动身去做留给他的那份工作：把我们北上之路一段段断开的线缕捻接起来，好让我们这些跟在他身后的人得以安然循行。

巴特利特船长回程的详细经过，我从未听人讲起过；他的因纽特小伙子们讲不出个所以然，而巴特利特船长本人又实在谦逊过了头。

## XIX / 十九

===Original===
The Roosevelt starts for home﻿—Eskimo villages﻿—New dogs and new dog fights.
It was two-thirty p.m., July 17, 1909, that the *Roosevelt* pointed her bow southward and we left our winter quarters and Cape Sheridan. We were on our journey home, all hands as happy as when, a year previous, we had started on our way north, with the added satisfaction of complete success. The ship had steamed but a short distance, when, owing to the rapidly drifting ice in the channel, she had to be made fast to a floeberg. At ten-thirty p.m., the lines were loosed and a new start made. Without further incident, we reached Black Cape.
In rounding the cape the ship encountered a terrific storm, and it was with the greatest difficulty that she made any headway. The storm increased and the *Roosevelt* had to remain in the channel, surrounded by the tightly wedged floes, at the mercy of the wind. The gale continued until the evening of the 20th. The constant surging back and forth of the channel-pack, with the spring tides and the several huge masses of ice, which repeatedly crashed against the ship’s sides, caused a delay of twelve days in Robeson Channel opposite Lincoln Bay. Throughout the width of the entire channel nothing could be seen but small pools of open water; two seals were seen sporting in one of these pools, and one of the Eskimos attempted to kill them, but his aim proved false.
It was not until the 25th that the ship was able to move of her own free will, small leads having opened in close proximity to her. Ootah shot a seal in one of the leads, and also harpooned a narwhal, but he did not succeed in securing either. His brother Egingwah on the following day shot two seals and harpooned a narwhal, and he secured all three of his prizes. The Eskimos had a grand feast off the skin of the narwhal, which they esteem as a great delicacy.
By the 27th the *Roosevelt* had drifted as far south as Wrangell Bay, and it was here that Slocum (Inighito) shot and secured a hood-seal, which weighed over six hundred pounds, and seal-steaks were added to the bill-of-fare.

===Chinese===
罗斯福号启程返家——因纽特人的村落——新来的狗和新的狗斗。
1909年7月17日下午两点半，*罗斯福号*调转船头向南，我们离开了越冬驻泊地和谢里登角。我们踏上了归程，全船上下快活得很，同一年前北上启程时一般无二，只是更添了一份大功告成的满足。船开出去没有多远，便因为海峡里浮冰漂流得太快，不得不系牢在一座浮冰山旁。到晚上十点半，缆绳解开，重新启航。此后一路无事，我们抵达了黑角（Black Cape）。
绕过海角时，船遇上一场狂暴的风暴，费了九牛二虎之力才勉强前进。风暴愈演愈烈，*罗斯福号*只得滞留海峡之中，被四周挤得死紧的浮冰团团围住，任凭风摆布。大风一直刮到20日傍晚。海峡里的浮冰群随着大潮来回涌动，又有几大块冰一次又一次撞上船舷，害得我们在罗伯逊海峡、林肯湾对面的水面上耽搁了十二天。整条海峡的宽度之内，除了零星几小片开阔水面，什么也看不见；有两只海豹在其中一片水洼里嬉水，一个因纽特人想猎杀它们，却失了准头。
直到25日，紧挨着船边裂开了几条小水道，船才得以凭自己的意志行动。乌塔在其中一条水道里射中一只海豹，又叉中一头一角鲸，可惜两样都没能弄到手。他的兄弟埃金瓦第二天射得两只海豹、叉中一头一角鲸，三样战利品尽数到手。因纽特人们拿一角鲸皮美美地大吃了一顿，那在他们看来是顶了不起的美味。
到27日，*罗斯福号*已向南漂到弗兰格尔湾（Wrangell Bay）。斯洛克姆（伊尼希托）就是在这里射得并捞起一只冠海豹，重达六百多磅，海豹排由此上了菜单。

===Original===
The snow storms of the two days ceased on the 28th, and when the weather cleared sufficiently for us to ascertain our whereabouts, we were much surprised to find that we had drifted back north, opposite Lincoln Bay. During the day the wind shifted to the north. Again we drifted southward, until, just off Cape Beechey, the narrowest part of Robeson Channel, a lead stretching southward for a distance of five miles was sighted, and into this open water the ship steamed until the lead terminated in Kennedy Channel, opposite Lady Franklin Bay, where the *Roosevelt* was icebound until August 4, drifting with the pack until we were in a direct line with Cape Tyson and Bellot Isle. Three seals were captured, one a hood-seal weighing 624 pounds, being eight feet eleven inches in length; the other two were small ring-seals.
By ten a.m. of the 4th, the ice had slackened so considerably that the *Roosevelt*, under full steam, set out and rapidly worked her way down Kennedy Channel. From Crozier Island to Cape D’Urville she steamed through practically open water, but a dense fog compelled us to make fast to a large floe when almost opposite Cape Albert. It was not until one a.m. of the 7th, despite several attempts, that the ship got clear and steamed south again. Several small leads were noticed and numerous narwhals were seen, but none were captured.
At three-thirty a.m., when nearing Cape Sabine, we observed that the barometer had dropped to 29.73. A storm was coming, and every effort was made to reach Payer Harbor, but before half of the distance had been covered, the storm broke with terrific violence. The force of the gale was such that, while swinging the boats inboard, we were drenched and thoroughly chilled by the sheets of icy spray, which saturated us and instantly froze. The *Roosevelt* was blown over to starboard until the rails were submerged. To save her, she was steered into Buchanan Bay, under the lee of the cliffs, where she remained until the morning of August 8.
At an early hour, we steamed down Buchanan Bay, passed Cocked Hat Island, and a little later, Cape Sabine. At Cape Sabine was located Camp Clay, the starvation camp of the Lady Franklin Bay expedition of 1881﻿–﻿1883, where the five survivors of the twenty-three members of the expedition were rescued.

===Chinese===
接连两天的暴风雪到28日停息了。等天气放晴到足以辨明我们的所在，大家吃惊地发现，我们竟又向北漂了回去，正对着林肯湾。白天里，风转向北面。我们再度向南漂移，直到罗伯逊海峡最窄处的比奇角（Cape Beechey）外，望见一条向南延展五英里的水道，船便驶进这片开阔水，一路开到水道在肯尼迪海峡内到了头、正对富兰克林夫人湾的地方。*罗斯福号*在那里被冰封到8月4日，其间随浮冰群漂移，最后与泰森角（Cape Tyson）和贝洛岛（Bellot Isle）连成了一条直线。被困期间捕得三只海豹：一只冠海豹重624磅，身长八英尺十一英寸；另两只是小个的环斑海豹。
4日上午十点，冰已松动得厉害，*罗斯福号*开足蒸汽启程，顺着肯尼迪海峡迅速南下。从克罗泽岛（Crozier Island）到迪尔维尔角（Cape D’Urville），船几乎一路行驶在开阔水面上；可是浓雾迫使我们差不多行至艾伯特角（Cape Albert）对面时，把船系牢到一大片浮冰上。尽管几度设法，直到7日凌晨一点，船才脱出身来，重新向南开行。途中注意到几条小水道，还看见许多一角鲸，只是一头也没有捕到。
凌晨三点半，临近萨拜恩角时，我们看到气压计降到了29.73。风暴就要来了，我们千方百计想赶到帕耶港（Payer Harbor），可是还没走完一半路程，风暴便以骇人的狂暴之势袭来。风势之猛，使得我们在把小艇吊进舷内的当口，被一片片冰冷的水雾浇得透湿、寒气直沁骨髓；水雾浸透了周身，转眼冻结成冰。*罗斯福号*被吹得向右舷倾侧，连栏杆都没入了水中。为了保住船，我们把她驶进布坎南湾，躲到峭壁的背风面下，在那里一直停到8月8日早晨。
一早，我们沿布坎南湾下行，驶过三角帽岛（Cocked Hat Island），不多时又过了萨拜恩角。萨拜恩角就是克莱营（Camp Clay）的所在——1881至1883年富兰克林夫人湾探险队断粮挨饿的营地，全队二十三名成员中幸存的五人就是在那里获救的。

===Original===
We entered Smith Sound. Instead of sailing on to Etah, Peary ordered the ship into Whale Sound, in order that walrus-hunting could be done, so that the Eskimos should have a plentiful supply of meat for the following winter. Three walrus were captured, when a storm sprang up with all of the suddenness of storms in this neighborhood, and the ship crossed over from Cape Alexander to Cape Chalon. Cape Chalon is a favorite resort of the Eskimos, and is known as Peter-ar-wick, on account of the walrus that are to be found here during the months of February and March.
At Nerke, just below Cape Chalon, we found the three Eskimo families of Ahsayoo, Tungwingwah, and Teddylingwah, and it was from these people we first learned of Dr. Cook’s safe return from Ellesmere Land. In spite of the fact that the *Roosevelt* was overloaded with dogs, paraphernalia, and Eskimos, these three families were taken aboard.
With them were several teams of dogs. The dogs aboard ship were the survivors of the pack that had been with us all through the campaign, and a number of litters of puppies that had been whelped since the spring season. Our dogs were well acquainted with each other and dog fights were infrequent and of little interest, but the arrival of the first dog of the new party was the signal for the grandest dog fight I have ever witnessed. I feel justified in using the language of the fairy Ariel, in Shakespeare’s “Tempest”: “Now is Hell empty, and all the devils are here.”

===Chinese===
我们驶入史密斯海峡。皮尔里没有让船径直开往埃塔，而是下令驶进鲸湾，以便猎捕海象，让因纽特人来年冬天有一份充裕的肉食。捕得三头海象之后，一场风暴骤然而起——这一带的风暴向来来得就是这样突然——船便从亚历山大角（Cape Alexander）渡海到了沙隆角（Cape Chalon）。沙隆角是因纽特人最爱落脚的去处，地名彼得拉维克（Peter-ar-wick），因为每年二月和三月间总有海象聚在此地。
在沙隆角往下一点的内尔克（Nerke），我们遇见了阿萨尤（Ahsayoo）、通温瓦（Tungwingwah）、特迪林瓦（Teddylingwah）三户因纽特人家；我们最先正是从他们嘴里听说，库克医生已从埃尔斯米尔地平安归来。尽管*罗斯福号*上的狗、辎重和因纽特人早已载得超负荷，这三户人家还是被接上了船。
他们还带着好几队狗。船上原有的狗，是随我们走完全程的那群狗中的幸存者，外加开春以来产下的几窝小狗。我们的狗彼此熟稔，狗打架的事难得发生，打起来也少有看头；可新来那伙人的第一条狗一上船，便成了我所见过的最盛大一场狗斗的开场信号。我觉得，借用莎士比亚《暴风雨》里精灵爱丽儿（Ariel）的话来说正合适：“地狱已经空了，群魔都在这里。”

===Original===
Backward and forward, the foredeck of the ship was a howling, snarling, biting, yelping, moving mass of fury, and it was a long round of fully ten or fifteen minutes before the two king dogs of the packs got together, and then began the battle for supremacy of the pack. It lasted for some time. It would have been useless to separate them. They would decide sooner or later, and it was better to have it over, even if one or both contestants were killed. At length the fight was ended; our old king dog, Nalegaksoah, the champion of the pack, and the laziest dog in it, was still the king. After vanquishing his opponent and receiving humble acknowledgments, King Nalegaksoah went stamping up and down before the pack and received the homage due him; the new dogs, whining and fawning and cringingly submissive, bowed down before him.
The chief pleasure of the Eskimo dogs is fighting; two dogs, the best of friends, will hair-pull and bite each other for no cause whatever, and strange dogs fight at sight; teammates fight each other on the slightest of provocations; and it seems as though sometimes the fights are held for the purpose of educating the young. When a fight is in progress, it is the usual sight to see several mother dogs, with their litters, occupying ringside seats. I have often wondered what chance a cat would stand against an Eskimo dog.
The ship kept on, and I had turned in and slept, and on arising had found that we had reached a place called Igluduhomidy, where a single family was located. Living with this family was a very old Eskimo, Merktoshah, the oldest man in the whole tribe, and not a blood-relation to any member of it. He had crossed over from the west coast of Smith Sound the same year that Hall’s expedition had wintered there, and has lived there ever since. He had been a champion polar bear and big game hunter, and though now a very old man, was still vigorous and valiant, in spite of the loss of one eye.
We stopped at Kookan, the most prosperous of the Eskimo settlements, a village of five tupiks (skin tents), housing twenty-four people, and from there we sailed to the ideal community of Karnah. Karnah is the most delightful spot on the Greenland coast. Situated on a gently southward sloping knoll are the igloos and tupiks, where I have spent many pleasant days with my Eskimo friends and learned much of the folklore and history. Lofty mountains, sublime in their grandeur, overtower and surround this place, and its only exposure is southward toward the sun. In winter its climate is not severe, as compared with other portions of this country, and in the perpetual daylight of summer, life here is ideal. Rivulets of clear, cold water, the beds of which are grass- and flower-covered, run down the sides of the mountains and, but for the lack of trees, the landscape is as delightful as anywhere on earth.

===Chinese===
船头甲板上，前前后后滚着一团狂怒的东西，嗥着、龇着、咬着、吠着，蠢蠢而动；这样足足闹了十到十五分钟，两群狗的王这才撞到一起，随即开始了争夺狗群霸主之位的战斗。这一仗打了一阵。要把它们分开是徒劳的。迟早总要见分晓，不如打完了事，哪怕一方或双方丧命也罢。末了，战斗结束；我们的老狗王纳莱加克索阿（Nalegaksoah）——狗群里的冠军，也是狗群里最懒的一条——依旧是王。战胜对手、受了对方低首下心的服输之后，纳莱加克索阿王便在狗群前面踏着重步来回踱着，受它应得的臣服之礼；新来的狗呜咽着、献着媚、卑怯地顺从，在它面前一个个伏低了身子。
因纽特狗最大的乐事就是打架：两条狗即便好得形影不离，也会无缘无故地互相扯毛咬嘴；生狗一照面就斗；同队拉橇的伙伴，稍受些微冒犯便掐起来；而且有时候，那架简直像是特意打来给年幼的受教育的。每当斗得正酣，常见的光景是几只母狗带着自家的崽子占着场边的位子观战。我常常纳闷，一只猫若同因纽特狗交手，能有几分胜算。
船继续前行。我上床睡了一觉，起身时发现我们已经到了一处叫伊格卢杜霍米迪（Igluduhomidy）的地方，那里只住着一户人家。同这户人家住在一起的，有一位年迈的因纽特老人默克托沙（Merktoshah），他是全部落里最年长的人，同部落中任何成员都没有血缘关系。当年霍尔探险队在史密斯海峡西岸越冬，他就是在那一年从西岸渡海过来的，此后一直住在这里。他从前是猎北极熊和大型猎物的顶尖好手，如今虽已年迈，又失了一只眼睛，却依旧筋骨硬朗、胆气不减。
我们在库坎（Kookan）停靠——那是因纽特人聚居地中最兴旺的一处，一个由五顶图皮克（tupiks，兽皮帐篷）组成、住着二十四口人的村落——随后从那里驶往堪称理想聚落的卡尔纳（Karnah）。卡尔纳是格陵兰海岸最讨人喜欢的地方。伊格鲁和图皮克坐落在一座缓缓向南倾斜的小丘上，我曾在那里同因纽特朋友们度过许多快活的日子，听来不少民间传说和史话。高峻的群山雄伟壮丽，高高耸峙，把这个地方四面围拢，只有朝南一面敞向太阳。同这地方的其他各处相比，这里冬天的气候算不得严酷；到了夏日永昼时分，此间的生活直如仙境。一道道清冽的溪水顺着山坡淌下，溪床上长满青草野花；若不是缺少树木，这景致同世上任何地方相比都不逊色。

## XV / 十五

===Original===
The Pole!
Captain Bartlett and his two boys had commenced their return journey, and the main column, depleted to its final strength, started northward. We were six: Peary, the commander, the Eskimos, Ootah, Egingwah, Seegloo and Ooqueah, and myself.
Day and night were the same. My thoughts were on the going and getting forward, and on nothing else. The wind was from the southeast, and seemed to push us on, and the sun was at our backs, a ball of livid fire, rolling his way above the horizon in never-ending day.
The Captain had gone, Commander Peary and I were alone (save for the four Eskimos), the same as we had been so often in the past years, and as we looked at each other we realized our position and we knew without speaking that the time had come for us to demonstrate that we were the men who, it had been ordained, should unlock the door which held the mystery of the Arctic. Without an instant’s hesitation, the order to push on was given, and we started off in the trail made by the Captain to cover the Farthest North he had made and to push on over one hundred and thirty miles to our final destination.

===Chinese===
北极点！
巴特利特船长带着他的两个小伙子动身返程，削减至最后一击实力的主力纵队向北开拔了。我们一共六人：皮尔里指挥官，因纽特人乌塔、埃金瓦、西格鲁和乌奎亚，再加上我。
白昼与黑夜已无分别。我心里只想着赶路、向前，再无别念。风从东南方来，仿佛推着我们走；太阳悬在我们背后，一团青白的火球，在无尽的长昼里沿着地平线滚动着它的行程。
船长走了，皮尔里指挥官和我单独相对（那四名因纽特人除外），一如过去那些年里我们常常的那样。我们对望一眼，都明白自己的处境；不用开口我们也知道，时刻已经到了：该由我们来证明，我们正是那命中注定要打开北极奥秘之门的人。推进的命令毫不犹豫地下了，我们顺着船长开出的路迹出发，去走完他抵达「最北点」的那段路，再向前推进一百三十多英里，直抵我们最后的目的地。

===Original===
The Captain had had rough going, but, owing to the fact that his trail was our track for a short time, and that we came to good going shortly after leaving his turning point, we made excellent distance without any trouble, and only stopped when we came to a lead barely frozen over, a full twenty-five miles beyond. We camped and waited for the strong southeast wind to force the sides of the lead together. The Eskimos had eaten a meal of stewed dog, cooked over a fire of wood from a discarded sledge, and, owing to their wonderful powers of recuperation, were in good condition; Commander Peary and myself, rested and invigorated by our thirty hours in the last camp, waiting for the return and departure of Captain Bartlett, were also in fine fettle, and accordingly the accomplishment of twenty-five miles of northward progress was not exceptional. With my proven ability in gauging distances, Commander Peary was ready to take the reckoning as I made it and he did not resort to solar observations until we were within a hand’s grasp of the Pole.
The memory of those last five marches, from the Farthest North of Captain Bartlett to the arrival of our party at the Pole, is a memory of toil, fatigue, and exhaustion, but we were urged on and encouraged by our relentless commander, who was himself being scourged by the final lashings of the dominating influence that had controlled his life. From the land to 87° 48′ north, Commander Peary had had the best of the going, for he had brought up the rear and had utilized the trail made by the preceding parties, and thus he had kept himself in the best of condition for the time when he made the spurt that brought him to the end of the race. From 87° 48′ north, he kept in the lead and did his work in such a way as to convince me that he was still as good a man as he had ever been. We marched and marched, falling down in our tracks repeatedly, until it was impossible to go on. We were forced to camp, in spite of the impatience of the Commander, who found himself unable to rest, and who only waited long enough for us to relax into sound sleep, when he would wake us up and start us off again. I do not believe that he slept for one hour from April 2 until after he had loaded us up and ordered us to go back over our old trail, and I often think that from the instant when the order to return was given until the land was again sighted, he was in a continual daze.
Onward we forced our weary way. Commander Peary took his sights from the time our chronometer-watches gave, and I, knowing that we had kept on going in practically a straight line, was sure that we had more than covered the necessary distance to insure our arrival at the top of the earth.

===Chinese===
船长那一段走得艰难；但他的路迹我们只循行了很短一程，而且过了他的折返点不久，我们便走上了好走的冰面，一路畅行无阻，赶出了极好的路程，直到遇上一条刚封上一层薄冰的水道才停下来——那已经又往北足足二十五英里。我们扎下营，等候强劲的东南风把水道两侧的冰挤拢。因纽特人用废弃雪橇的木料生火，炖了一顿狗肉吃下肚，凭着惊人的恢复力，个个状态甚佳；皮尔里指挥官和我，在上一座营地里歇了三十个钟头，一面等候巴特利特船长的归来与离去，一面养足了精神，也同样状态极佳；因此，一天北进二十五英里并不算出格。有我那经过检验的估程本领，皮尔里指挥官乐意照我报的数目记账，直到我们离北极点近在咫尺，他才求助于太阳观测。
从巴特利特船长的「最北点」到我们一队抵达北极点，这最后五个行程留在记忆里的，唯有劳苦、疲惫与耗竭；但驱策我们、鼓舞我们的，是那位毫不容情的指挥官，而他自己，正受着那支配了他一生的主宰之力最后的鞭笞。从陆地到北纬87度48分，皮尔里指挥官走的都是最好的路，因为他一直殿后，利用前面各队开出的路迹，从而把自己保养得元气十足，只待最后冲刺、一举跑到这场竞赛终点的时刻到来。从北纬87度48分起，他一直领先开路，他那股干劲使我确信，他仍旧一如既往，是一条好汉。我们走了一程又一程，一再当场栽倒，直到再也走不动为止。尽管指挥官焦躁难耐，我们还是不得不扎营；他自己歇不下来，只等我们松弛到睡熟，便把我们叫醒，重新催我们上路。我相信，从4月2日起，直到他把我们装载停当、命我们沿旧路迹返回之后，他统共没睡上一个钟头；我常常想，从返程命令下达的那一刻起，直到重新望见陆地，他一直处在连绵不断的恍惚之中。
我们强拖着疲惫的身子向前赶路。皮尔里指挥官按我们精密时计表报出的时刻进行观测；而我呢，知道我们实际上一直在沿直线行进，确信我们走出的距离早已绰绰有余，足以保证我们抵达地球之巅。

===Original===
It was during the march of the 3rd of April that I endured an instant of hideous horror. We were crossing a lane of moving ice. Commander Peary was in the lead setting the pace, and a half hour later the four boys and myself followed in single file. They had all gone before, and I was standing and pushing at the upstanders of my sledge, when the block of ice I was using as a support slipped from underneath my feet, and before I knew it the sledge was out of my grasp, and I was floundering in the water of the lead. I did the best I could. I tore my hood from off my head and struggled frantically. My hands were gloved and I could not take hold of the ice, but before I could give the “Grand Hailing Sigh of Distress,” faithful old Ootah had grabbed me by the nape of the neck, the same as he would have grabbed a dog, and with one hand he pulled me out of the water, and with the other hurried the team across.
He had saved my life, but I did not tell him so, for such occurrences are taken as part of the day’s work, and the sledge he safeguarded was of much more importance, for it held, as part of its load, the Commander’s sextant, the mercury, and the coils of piano-wire that were the essential portion of the scientific part of the expedition. My kamiks (boots of sealskin) were stripped off, and the congealed water was beaten out of my bearskin trousers, and with a dry pair of kamiks, we hurried on to overtake the column. When we caught up, we found the boys gathered around the Commander, doing their best to relieve him of his discomfort, for he had fallen into the water also, and while he was not complaining, I was sure that his bath had not been any more voluntary than mine had been.
When we halted on April 6, 1909, and started to build the igloos, the dogs and sledges having been secured, I noticed Commander Peary at work unloading his sledge and unpacking several bundles of equipment. He pulled out from under his *kooletah* (thick, fur outer-garment) a small folded package and unfolded it. I recognized his old silk flag, and realized that this was to be a camp of importance. Our different camps had been known as Camp Number One, Number Two, etc., but after the turning back of Captain Bartlett, the camps had been given names such as Camp Nansen, Camp Cagni, etc., and I asked what the name of this camp was to be﻿—“Camp Peary”? “This, my boy, is to be Camp Morris K. Jesup, the last and most northerly camp on the earth.” He fastened the flag to a staff and planted it firmly on the top of his igloo. For a few minutes it hung limp and lifeless in the dead calm of the haze, and then a slight breeze, increasing in strength, caused the folds to straighten out, and soon it was rippling out in sparkling color. The stars and stripes were “nailed to the Pole.”

===Chinese===
正是在4月3日那一程，我经受了一瞬骇人的恐怖。我们正在横穿一条漂移的冰道。皮尔里指挥官在前面领头定着步调；半个钟头后，四个小伙子和我成一路纵队跟了上去。他们都已过去了，我正站在雪橇立柱后推着橇走，脚下当作支点的那块冰突然从脚底滑脱，不等我明白过来，雪橇已脱出手去，我已在那条水道的水里扑腾。我尽了最大的努力：扯掉头上的风帽，拼命挣扎。我双手戴着手套，抓不住冰面；可还没等我发出那「遇险求救的大呼号」（Grand Hailing Sigh of Distress），忠厚的老乌塔已经像抓一条狗似的攥住了我的后颈，一只手把我从水里拽了出来，另一只手催着狗队赶紧过了冰道。
他救了我一命，但我没有对他说什么，因为这类事都被当作当天的份内活计；他保住的那架雪橇要紧得多，因为橇上载着指挥官的六分仪、水银，还有几卷钢琴钢丝——那都是探险队科学工作中最紧要的部分。我的卡米克（kamiks，海豹皮靴）被扒了下来，熊皮裤上冻住的水也被敲打干净，换上一双干爽的卡米克，我们赶紧赶路去追大队。追上之后，只见小伙子们围在指挥官身边，正想方设法替他解除不适——原来他也落了水；他虽然没有抱怨，但我敢肯定，他那场沐浴同我的一样，绝非出于自愿。
1909年4月6日，我们停了下来，动手垒伊格鲁；狗和雪橇都已安置妥当，我看见皮尔里指挥官正在卸他的雪橇，解开几捆装备。他从*库利塔*（kooletah，厚毛外衣）底下掏出一个折叠的小包，把它展开。我认出了他那面旧丝旗，意识到这将是一座意义重大的营地。我们的各个营地先后叫作一号营地、二号营地等等；但自巴特利特船长折返之后，营地便都有了名号，如南森营地（Camp Nansen）、卡尼营地（Camp Cagni）等，于是我问这座营地要叫什么——「皮尔里营地」？「这一座啊，我的小伙子，要叫莫里斯·K·杰瑟普营地，地球上最后、也是最北的一座营地。」他把旗子系上一根旗杆，牢牢插在他的伊格鲁顶上。有那么几分钟，它蔫蔫地垂着，毫无生气地悬在雾靄的死寂里；随后一阵微风渐渐加强，吹得旗面舒展开来，不多时便波荡翻卷，闪出耀眼的色彩。星条旗「钉上了北极点」。

===Original===
A thrill of patriotism ran through me and I raised my voice to cheer the starry emblem of my native land. The Eskimos gathered around and, taking the time from Commander Peary, three hearty cheers rang out on the still, frosty air, our dumb dogs looking on in puzzled surprise. As prospects for getting a sight of the sun were not good, we turned in and slept, leaving the flag proudly floating above us.
This was a thin silk flag that Commander Peary had carried on all of his Arctic journeys, and he had always flown it at his last camps. It was as glorious and as inspiring a banner as any battle-scarred, bloodstained standard of the world﻿—and this badge of honor and courage was also bloodstained and battle-scarred, for at several places there were blank squares marking the spots where pieces had been cut out at each of the “Farthests” of its brave bearer, and left with the records in the cairns, as mute but eloquent witnesses of his achievements. At the North Pole a diagonal strip running from the upper left to the lower right corner was cut and this precious strip, together with a brief record, was placed in an empty tin, sealed up and buried in the ice, as a record for all time.
Commander Peary also had another American flag, sewn on a white ground, and it was the emblem of the “Daughters of the Revolution Peace Society”; he also had and flew the emblem of the Navy League, and the emblems of a couple of college fraternities of which he was a member.

===Chinese===
一股爱国的激流涌遍我的全身，我放开嗓门，为我祖国那缀满星辰的旗帜欢呼。因纽特人围拢过来，随着皮尔里指挥官的领呼，三声洪亮的欢呼在凝滞凛冽的空气里响起，我们那些不会说话的狗在一旁困惑地呆望着。眼见太阳的希望不大，我们便钻进睡袋睡了，任那面旗子在我们头顶骄傲地飘扬。
这是一面薄薄的丝旗，皮尔里指挥官历次北极之行都带着它，每逢最后的营地总要把它升起。它同世界上任何一面弹痕累累、血迹斑斑的战旗一样光荣、一样激动人心——而这荣誉与勇气的徽记，也同样带着血迹与战痕：旗面上有好几处空白的方块，标示着它那勇敢的执旗人每创下一处「最北点」，便从那里裁下一小块，同记录一起留在石堆里，作为他业绩无言而雄辩的见证。在北极点，又从左上角到右下角斜裁下一条；这宝贵的旗条同一份简短的记录一起，装进一只空铁罐，封了口，埋进冰里，作为传之永久的记录。
皮尔里指挥官还有另一面美国国旗，缝在一块白底上，那是「革命女儿会和平协会」（Daughters of the Revolution Peace Society）的徽记；他还带着并升起了海军联合会（Navy League）的会徽，以及他所属的两个大学兄弟会的会徽。

===Original===
It was about ten or ten-thirty a.m., on the 7th of April, 1909, that the Commander gave the order to build a snow-shield to protect him from the flying drift of the surface-snow. I knew that he was about to take an observation, and while we worked I was nervously apprehensive, for I felt that the end of our journey had come. When we handed him the pan of mercury the hour was within a very few minutes of noon. Laying flat on his stomach, he took the elevation and made the notes on a piece of tissue-paper at his head. With sun-blinded eyes, he snapped shut the vernier (a graduated scale that subdivides the smallest divisions on the sector of the circular scale of the sextant) and with the resolute squaring of his jaws, I was sure that he was satisfied, and I was confident that the journey had ended. Feeling that the time had come, I ungloved my right hand and went forward to congratulate him on the success of our eighteen years of effort, but a gust of wind blew something into his eye, or else the burning pain caused by his prolonged look at the reflection of the limb of the sun forced him to turn aside; and with both hands covering his eyes, he gave us orders to not let him sleep for more than four hours, for six hours later he purposed to take another sight about four miles beyond, and that he wanted at least two hours to make the trip and get everything in readiness.
I unloaded a sledge, and reloaded it with a couple of skins, the instruments, and a cooker with enough alcohol and food for one meal for three, and then I turned in to the igloo where my boys were already sound asleep. The thermometer registered 29° below zero. I fell into a dreamless sleep and slept for about a minute, so I thought, when I was awakened by the clatter and noise made by the return of Peary and his boys.
The Commander gave the word, “We will plant the stars and stripes﻿—*at the North Pole!*” and it was done; on the peak of a huge paleocrystic floeberg the glorious banner was unfurled to the breeze, and as it snapped and crackled with the wind, I felt a savage joy and exultation. Another world’s accomplishment was done and finished, and as in the past, from the beginning of history, wherever the world’s work was done by a white man, he had been accompanied by a colored man. From the building of the pyramids and the journey to the Cross, to the discovery of the new world and the discovery of the North Pole, the Negro had been the faithful and constant companion of the Caucasian, and I felt all that it was possible for me to feel, that it was I, a lowly member of my race, who had been chosen by fate to represent it, at this, almost the last of the world’s great *work*.

===Chinese===
1909年4月7日上午十点或十点半光景，指挥官下令垒一道挡雪墙，好护住他免遭地表积雪随风刮起的飞打。我知道他就要进行观测了；我们干活的时候，我心里紧张得七上八下，因为我感到我们旅程的尽头到了。我们把水银盘递到他手里时，离正午只差几分钟了。他全身伏在地上，测了高度，在头边的一张薄纸上记下数据。他用被阳光照花的双眼啪的一声合上游标（vernier，一种刻度尺，能把六分仪圆弧刻度上的最小分度再行细分），牙关果断地一咬；我确信他是满意的，也确信旅程已经到了头。我觉得时机已到，便褪下右手的手套，上前祝贺我们十八年辛劳换来的成功；可一阵风把什么东西吹进了他的眼睛，也许是长久凝视太阳边缘的倒影造成的灼痛迫使他转过头去；他双手捂着眼睛，吩咐我们别让他睡过四个钟头，因为六个钟头之后，他打算到前面约四英里处再作一次观测，而他至少要留出两个钟头赶路，并把一切准备停当。
我卸空一架雪橇，重新装上几张兽皮、那些仪器，还有一只炊具和够三个人吃一顿的酒精与食物，随后钻进伊格鲁，我的小伙子们早已睡熟了。温度计指着零下29度。我坠入无梦的酣睡，自以为只睡了约莫一分钟，就被皮尔里带着他的人回来时的喧闹响动吵醒了。
指挥官下了令：「我们要把星条旗插上——*就在北极点！*」于是办到了；在一座巨大古浮冰山的顶峰上，那面光荣的旗帜迎风展开，在风中猎猎作响，我心头涌起一阵狂野的喜悦与欢腾。又一桩世界的伟业完成、做成了；而有史以来，凡有世界的功业由白人做成之处，他身旁总伴随着一个有色人种。从金字塔的建造、十字军的远征，到新大陆的发现和北极点的发现，黑人一直是白种人忠实而恒久的同伴；而我心中感受到了我所能感受的一切：在这几乎是世界最后一桩伟大*事业*上，被命运选中代表我种族的，竟是我——我种族中一个卑微的成员。

===Original===
The four Eskimos who stood with Commander Peary at the North Pole, were the brothers, Ootah and Egingwah, the old campaigner, Seegloo, and the sturdy, boyish Ooqueah. Four devoted companions, blindly confident in the leader, they worked only that he might succeed and for the promise of reward that had been made before they had left the ship, which promise they were sure would be kept. Together with the faithful dogs, these men had insured the success of the master. They had all of the characteristics of the dogs, including the dogs’ fidelity. Within their breasts lingered the same infatuations that Commander Peary seemed to inspire in all who were with him, and though frequently complaining and constantly requiring to be urged to do their utmost, they worked faithfully and willingly. Ootah, of my party, was the oldest, a married man, of about thirty-four years, and regarded as the best all around member of the tribe, a great hunter, a kind father, and a good provider. Owing to his strong character and the fact that he was more easily managed by me than by any of the others, he had been a member of my party from the time we left the ship. Without exaggeration, I can say that we had both saved each other’s lives more than once, but it had all gone in as part of the day’s work, and neither of us dwelt on our obligations to the other.
My other boy, Ooqueah, was a young man of about nineteen or twenty, very sturdy and stocky of build, and with an open, honest countenance, a smile that was “childlike and bland,” and a character that *was* childlike and bland. It was alleged that the efforts of young Ooqueah were spurred on by the shafts of love, and that it was in the hopes of winning the hand of the demure Miss Anadore, the charming daughter of Ikwah, the first Eskimo of Commander Peary’s acquaintance, that he worked so valiantly. His efforts were of an ardent character, but it was not due to the ardor of love, as far as I could see, but to his desire to please and his anxiety to win the promised rewards that would raise him to the grade of a millionaire, according to Eskimo standards.
Commander Peary’s boy, Egingwah, was the brother of my boy Ootah, also married and of good report in his community, and it was he who drove the Morris K. Jesup sledge.
If there was any sentiment among the Eskimos in regard to the success of the venture, Ootah and Seegloo by their unswerving loyalty and fidelity expressed it. They had been members of the “Farthest North party” in 1906, the party that was almost lost beyond and in the “Big Lead,” and only reached the land again in a state of almost complete collapse. They were the ones who, on bidding Commander Peary farewell in 1906, when he was returning, a saddened and discouraged man, told him to be of good cheer and that when he came back again Ootah and Seegloo would go along, and stay until Commander Peary had succeeded, and they did. The cowardice of their fellow Eskimos at the “Big Lead” on this journey did not in the least demoralize them, and when they were absolutely alone on the trail, with every chance to turn back and return to comfort, wife, and family, they remained steadfast and true, and ever northward guided their sledges.

===Chinese===
同皮尔里指挥官一起站在北极点上的四名因纽特人，是乌塔和埃金瓦兄弟、久经阵仗的老手西格鲁，还有体格结实、孩子气犹存的乌奎亚。四个忠心耿耿的伙伴，对领袖怀着盲目的信赖；他们劳作，只为让他成功，只为着离船之前许下的那份赏格——而他们确信这许诺必将兑现。连同那些忠实的狗，这几个人保证了主人此行的成功。他们身上兼备狗的种种品性，连狗的那种忠贞也包括在内。皮尔里指挥官似乎能让所有随行者心生同一种痴迷，他们的胸中也同样萦绕着这份痴迷；他们虽然时常抱怨，时时要人督促才肯拿出全力，干起活来却忠实而甘愿。我队里的乌塔年纪最长，是个成了家的人，约莫三十四岁，被公认为部族中最全面的人才：了不起的猎人，慈爱的父亲，养家糊口的一把好手。由于他性子刚强，又由于比起旁人来他更听我的调遣，从离船那一刻起，他就一直在我队里。可以毫不夸张地说，我们俩互相救过的命不止一回；可这些都当作日常份内的活计消化掉了，谁也不把自己欠对方的情记挂在心。
我的另一个小伙子乌奎亚，是个十九二十岁的年轻人，身板结实敦实，面容开朗诚实，一副「天真而温和」的笑容，性子也*确实*天真温和。据说年轻的乌奎亚干劲十足，是中了爱神的箭；他这样奋勇出力，是指望赢得娴静的阿纳多尔小姐（Anadore）的芳心——她是伊克瓦（Ikwah）的可爱女儿，而伊克瓦正是皮尔里指挥官结识的第一位因纽特人。他的努力确实热烈，但依我看，动力并非爱情的火热，而是想讨人欢喜的心思，加上一心要赢得那许下的赏赐——按因纽特人的标准，那赏赐足以把他抬进百万富翁的行列。
皮尔里指挥官的小伙子埃金瓦，是我手下乌塔的兄弟，也已娶妻，在乡邻中名声很好；赶莫里斯·K·杰瑟普那架雪橇的，正是他。
若说因纽特人对此番壮举的成败还怀有什么情义，乌塔和西格鲁已用毫不动摇的忠诚与信义把它表达了出来。他们俩曾是1906年「最北点队伍」的成员；那支队伍在「大水道」及其以远几乎全军覆没，重新踏上陆地时已是一副近乎彻底垮掉的架子。1906年皮尔里指挥官满怀忧伤、灰心丧气地返航时，正是他们俩在道别之际劝他宽心，说等他再来时，乌塔和西格鲁一定随行，一直陪到他成功为止——他们确实做到了。此行在「大水道」边上，同行的因纽特人那番怯懦丝毫没有挫动他们的心志；当他们在路迹上彻底孤身、明明处处有机会折返回去、重回安逸和妻子家人身边时，他们依然坚定不移、忠贞不渝，引导着雪橇永远向北。

## XVI / 十六

===Original===
The fast trek back to land.
The long trail was finished, the work was done, and there was only left for us to return and tell the tale of the doing. Reaction had set in, and it was with quavering voice that Commander Peary gave the order to break camp. Already the strain of the hard upward-journey was beginning to tell, and after the first two marches back, he was practically a dead weight, but do not think that we could have gotten back without him, for it was due to the fact that he was with us, and that we could depend upon him to direct and order us, that we were able to keep up the breakneck pace that enabled us to cover three of our upward marches on one of our return marches, and we never forgot that he was still the heart and head of the party.
It was broad daylight and getting brighter, and accordingly I knew little fear, though I did think of the ghosts of other parties, flitting in spectral form over the ice-clad wastes, especially of that small detachment of the Italian expedition of the Duke d’Abruzzi, of which to this day neither track, trace, nor remembrance has ever been found. We crossed lead after lead, sometimes like a bareback rider in the circus, balancing on cake after cake of ice, but good fortune was with us all of the way, and it was not until the land of recognizable character had been lifted that we lost the trail, and with the land in sight as an incentive, it was no trouble for us to gain the talus of the shore ice and find the trail again.
When we “hit the beach for fair” it was early in the morning of April 23, 1909, nearly seventeen days since we had left the Pole, but such a seventeen days of haste, toil, and misery as cannot be comprehended by the mind. We who experienced it, Commander Peary, the Eskimos, and myself, look back to it as to a horrid nightmare, and to describe it is impossible for me.

===Chinese===
疾速跋涉返回陆地。
漫长的征途已经走完，工作已经告成，留给我们的，只剩下回去讲述这段经历了。此时身心松弛的反应已然袭来，皮尔里指挥官下达拔营命令时，声音都在发颤。艰苦北上之行积下的劳损，已经开始在他身上显露，返程头两程走下来，他实际上已成了一副死沉的担子；但千万不要以为，没有他我们照样回得来——正因为有他同我们在一起，正因为我们能仰仗他指引调度、发号施令，我们才维持得住那种不要命的速度，才做得到在返程的一程里赶完北上的三程。我们从未忘记：他依然是这支队伍的心脏与头脑。
当时已是一片白昼，而且天色越来越亮，因此我心里并不怎么害怕，尽管我确实想到过其他队伍的亡魂——他们化作幽灵，在冰封的荒原上飘荡——尤其想到阿布鲁齐公爵（Duke d’Abruzzi）意大利探险队的那一小队人马：他们的踪迹、痕迹乃至丝毫可供追念之物，至今从未被发现。我们越过一条又一条水道，有时像马戏团里骑无鞍马的艺人，在一块又一块浮冰上努力保持平衡；但好运一路相随，直到那轮廓可辨的陆地升起在视野中，我们才走失了路线；有陆地在望作指引，我们便毫不费事地登上岸冰的斜坡，重新找到了路。
等我们确确实实「踏上滩头」的时候，已是1909年4月23日的清晨——离开极点将近十七天了；可那是怎样的十七天啊：那样的仓促、劳苦与悲惨，绝非人的心智所能领会。我们这些亲历者——皮尔里指挥官、因纽特人和我——回首那段日子，就像回望一场可怖的噩梦；要我把它描述出来，是不可能的。

===Original===
Commander Peary had taken the North Pole by conquest, in the face of almost insuperable natural difficulties, by the tremendous fighting-power of himself. The winning of the North Pole was a fight with nature; the way to the Pole that had been covered and retraced by Commander Peary lay across the ever moving and drifting ice of the Arctic Ocean. For more than a hundred miles from Cape Columbia it was piled in heavy pressure ridges, ridge after ridge, some more than a hundred feet in height. In addition, open lanes of water held the parties back until the leads froze up again, and continually the steady drift of the ice carried us back on the course we had come, but due to his deathless ambition to know and to do, he had conquered. He had added to the sum of Earth’s knowledge, and proven that the mind of man is boundless in its desire.
The long quest for the North Pole is over and the awful space that separated man from the Ultima Thule has been bridged. There is no more beyond; from Cape Columbia to Cape Chelyuskin, the route northward to the Pole, and southward again to the plains of Asia, is an open book and the geographical mind is at rest.
We found the abandoned igloos of Crane City and realized that Captain Bartlett had reached the land safely. The damage due to the action of the storms was not material. We made the necessary repairs, and in a few minutes tea was boiled and rations eaten, and we turned in for sleep. For practically all of the two days following, that was what we did: sleep and eat; men and dogs thoroughly exhausted; and we slept the sleep of the just, without apprehensions or misgivings. Our toboggan from the Pole was ended.

===Chinese===
皮尔里指挥官面对几乎无法克服的自然险阻，凭着自身惊人的战斗力量，以征服之势拿下了北极点。赢得北极点是一场与自然的搏斗；皮尔里指挥官往返踏过的那条通往极点的路，横贯在北冰洋永不停息地移动、漂移的浮冰之上。从哥伦比亚角起的一百多英里，冰层挤压堆叠成厚重的冰脊，一脊接一脊，有些高达一百多英尺。此外，一条条开阔的水道把各队人马拦在半路，直到水道重新封冻方能前进；而冰层持续不断的漂流，又一再把我们朝着来路的方向拽回去。但是，凭着那份求索与实干的不灭雄心，他终于征服了一切。他为地球知识的总和添上了新的一笔，也证明了人的心智在其追求上永无止境。
人类对北极点的漫长求索到此结束，隔在人类与极北终极之境（Ultima Thule）之间的那段可畏的空间，也已被跨越。极北之外，再无更远之地；从哥伦比亚角到切柳斯金角（Cape Chelyuskin），北抵极点、再折向南直下亚洲平原的这条路，如今已是一部敞开的书，地理学界的心也随之安定了。
我们找到了鹤城那些被弃置的伊格鲁，知道巴特利特船长已平安抵达陆地。风暴侵袭造成的损坏并不严重。我们做了必要的修缮，几分钟后茶煮好了，口粮吃下了，便躺下睡觉。此后差不多整整两天，我们做的就是这些事：睡觉，吃饭；人和狗都精疲力竭；我们睡的是问心无愧的酣觉，没有忧虑，没有不安。我们从极点一路滑行而下的雪橇之旅，到此结束了。

===Original===
---
Different from all other trips, we had not on this one been maddened by the pangs of hunger, but instead we felt the effects of lack of sleep, and brain- and body-fatigue. After reaching the land again, I gave a keen searching look at each member of the party, and I realized the strain they had been under. Instead of the plump, round countenances I knew so well, I saw lean, gaunt faces, seamed and wrinkled, the faces of old men, not those of boys, but in their eyes still shone the spark of resolute determination.
Commander Peary’s face was lined and seamed, his beard was fully an inch in length, and his mustaches, which had been closely cropped before he left the ship, had again attained their full flowing length. His features expressed fatigue, but the heartbreaking look of sadness, that had clung to him since the failure of the 1906 expedition, had vanished. From his steel-gray eyes flashed forth the light of glorious victory, and though he always carried himself proudly, there had come about him an air of erect assurance that was exhilarating.
When I reached the ship again and gazed into my little mirror, it was the pinched and wrinkled visage of an old man that peered out at me, but the eyes still twinkled and life was still entrancing. This wizening of our features was due to the strain of travel and lack of sleep; we had enough to eat, and I have only mentioned it to help impress the fact that the journey to the Pole and back is not to be regarded as a pleasure outing, and our so-called jaunt was by no means a cakewalk.

===Chinese===
---
与以往所有行程都不同，这一次我们没有因饥饿的绞痛而发狂，感受到的却是缺觉以及脑力与体力双重疲劳的影响。重新回到陆地之后，我把队里的每一个人细细端详了一番，这才明白他们承受过怎样的重压。我再也看不到那些熟悉的丰润圆脸，映入眼帘的是一张张瘦削憔悴、布满皱纹的面孔——那是老人的脸，不是小伙子的脸；但在他们眼中，依然闪烁着坚毅决绝的火花。
皮尔里指挥官的脸上刻满纵横的皱纹，胡须足有一英寸长；离船前剪得短短的八字胡，又长回了原先飘逸的长度。他的面容透着疲惫，但自1906年探险失利以来一直缠着他不放的那种令人心碎的哀容，已经消失无踪。他那双钢灰色的眼睛里，闪射出辉煌胜利的光芒；他素来举止傲然，而此刻周身更添了一种挺拔自信的气度，令人为之振奋。
等我回到船上，对着我的小镜子一照，从镜中望出来打量我的，是一张干瘪起皱的老人面孔；但那双眼睛依然炯炯有神，生活依然令人心醉。我们面容的这种干枯憔悴，是旅途劳顿和睡眠不足所致；我们的吃食是足够的。我提这一笔，只是想让人更深地记住一个事实：往返北极点的旅程，绝不能当作一次逍遥出游，而我们所谓的远足，也绝非一桩轻松容易的事。

## XVII / 十七

===Original===
Safe on the Roosevelt﻿—Poor Marvin.
If you will remember, the journey from Cape Sheridan to Cape Columbia was with overloaded sledges in the darkness preceding the dawn of the Arctic day, mostly over rough going and uphill, and now the tables were turned. It was broad day and downhill with lightened sledges, so that we practically coasted the last miles from the twin peaks of Columbia to the low, slanting foreshore of Sheridan and the *Roosevelt*. After the forty hours’ rest at Cape Columbia, Commander Peary had his sledges loaded up, and with Egingwah and the best of the remaining dogs, he got away.
I was told I could remain at the camp for another twelve hours. A large and substantial cache of supplies had been dropped at Cape Columbia by various members of the expedition and when the Commander was gone, I gave the boys full permission to turn in and eat all they wanted, and I also gave the dogs all they could stuff, and it was not until all of us had gorged ourselves to repletion that I gave the order to vamoose. We were loaded to capacity, outward and inward, and we saw a bountiful supply still lying there, but we could not pack another ounce. It was early in the morning of April 25 when Peary started for the ship; it was about four or five hours later, about noon, when I gave the word, and Ootah, Seegloo, Ooqueah, and myself left Crane City, Cape Columbia, Grant Land, for the last time.

===Chinese===
平安回到罗斯福号——可怜的马文。
你如果还记得，从谢里登角到哥伦比亚角那一路，是赶着装载过重的雪橇、在北极白昼破晓之前的黑暗里行进，多半是崎岖难行的上坡路；如今情形整个倒了过来。正是朗朗白昼，雪橇轻了载，一路下坡，从哥伦比亚的双峰直到谢里登那低斜的前滩、直到*罗斯福号*跟前，最后这几英里我们简直是顺坡溜下来的。在哥伦比亚角休整了四十个小时之后，皮尔里指挥官叫人把雪橇装好，带着埃金瓦和余下的狗里最好的一群，先行出发了。
我得到通知，还可以在营地里再待上十二个小时。探险队的各方人马先前在哥伦比亚角卸下了一大批殷实的补给储藏；等指挥官一走，我便放手让小伙子们大吃特吃，想吃多少吃多少，也让狗们吃到再也塞不进去；直到我们大家全都吃饱塞足，我才下令开拔。我们橇上装足、肚里塞满，里外都到了极限，眼看着还有一堆丰盛的补给躺在那里，却连一盎司也再装不下了。4月25日清晨，皮尔里动身返船；约莫四五个小时之后，将近正午，我一声令下，乌塔、西格鲁、乌奎亚和我，最后离开了格兰特地哥伦比亚角的鹤城。

===Original===
We overtook the Commander at Point Moss, and we traveled with him to Cape Colan, where we camped. Peary continued on to Sail Harbor, and we stayed in our comfortable camp and rested. We again caught up with the Commander at Porter Bay, where we camped for a few hours. The following morning I rearranged the sledges and left two of them at Porter Bay. It was my intention to reach the ship on this evening. We made a short stop at Black Cliff Bay and had lunch, and without further interruption we traveled on and at about eight-forty-five p.m. we sighted the *Roosevelt*.
The sighting of the ship was our first view of home, and far away as she was, our acutely developed senses of smell were regaled with the appetizing odor of hot coffee, and the pungent aroma of tobacco-smoke, wafted to us through the clear, germ-free air. The Eskimo boys, usually excited on the slightest provocation, were surprisingly stolid and merely remarked, “*Oomiaksoah*” (“The ship”) in quiet voices, until I, unable to control myself, burst forth with a loud “hip! hip! hurrah!” and with all that was left of my energy hurried my sledge in to the ship. We had been sighted almost as quickly as we had sighted the ship, and a party of the ship’s crew came running out to meet us, and as we rushed on we were told about the safe arrival of Commander Peary, Bartlett, Borup, MacMillan, and Dr. Goodsell. Transported with elation and overjoyed to find myself once more safe among friends, I had rushed onward and as I recognized the different faces of the ship’s company, I did not realize that some were missing.
Chief Wardwell was the first man to greet me, he photographed me as I was closing in on the ship, and with his strong right arm pulled me up over the side and hugged me to his bosom. “Good boy, Matt,” he said; “too bad about Marvin,” and then I knew that all was wrong and that it was not the time for rejoicing. I asked for Peary and I was told that he was all right. I saw Captain Bartlett and I knew that he was there; but where was Borup, where were MacMillan, Marvin, and where was Dr. Goodsell? Dr. Goodsell was right by my side, holding me up, and I realized that it was of him I was demanding to know of the others.

===Chinese===
我们在莫斯角（Point Moss）追上指挥官，同他一路走到科兰角，在那里宿营。皮尔里继续赶往塞尔港，我们便留在舒适的营地里歇息。到波特湾我们再次赶上指挥官，在那里扎营歇了几个小时。第二天早晨，我重新调配了雪橇，把其中两乘留在波特湾。我打算当天傍晚就回到船上。我们在黑崖湾（Black Cliff Bay）稍作停留，吃了午饭，此后一路再无耽搁，到晚上八点四十五分左右，我们望见了*罗斯福号*。
望见这条船，就是望见了家的第一眼。船还离得远远的，可我们磨砺得异常敏锐的嗅觉已经先受了用：热咖啡诱人的香味、烟草辛辣的气息，透过清冽无菌的空气，一阵阵飘送到我们跟前。因纽特小伙子们平日一星半点的由头就能兴奋起来，这会儿却出奇地木然，只低声说了句“乌米亚克索阿”（*Oomiaksoah*，即“船”）；直到我再也按捺不住，爆发出一声响亮的“嗨！嗨！乌拉！”，使出剩下的全部力气，催着雪橇直往船边赶去。几乎就在我们望见船的同时，船上的人也望见了我们，一队船员跑出来迎接我们；我们一路飞奔，一路听人说起皮尔里指挥官、巴特利特、博鲁普、麦克米伦和古德塞尔医生都已平安抵达。我欢喜得忘乎所以，为自己又一次安然回到朋友们中间而喜出望外，只顾一路往前冲；当我认出船上众人一张张不同的面孔时，竟没有察觉其中缺了几个人。
轮机长沃德维尔是第一个上来迎接我的人。我向船靠拢时，他给我拍了照，随即用他那条有力的右臂把我拽上船舷，紧紧搂进怀里。“好样的，马特，”他说，“马文的事太不幸了。”到这时我才明白一切都不对了，此刻不是欢庆的时候。我问起皮尔里，人们告诉我他一切安好。我看见了巴特利特船长，知道他在船上；可是博鲁普在哪儿？麦克米伦和马文在哪儿？古德塞尔医生又在哪儿？古德塞尔医生就在我身边搀扶着我，我这才意识到，我正向他追问别人下落的这个人，恰恰就是他。

===Original===
Reason had not left me, the bonds of sanity had not snapped, but for the time I was hysterical, and I only knew that all were well and safe excepting Marvin, who was drowned. A big mug of coffee was given to me, I drank a spoonful; a glass of spirits was handed me, I drank it all, and I was guided to my cabin, my fur clothes were taken off, and for the first time in sixty-eight days, I allowed myself to relax and I fell into a sleep.
When I awoke, I had the grandest feast imaginable set before me, and after eating, I had the most luxurious bath possible, and then some more to eat, and afterwards, some more sleep; then I shaved myself, combed my hair, and came out of my cabin and crossed over to the galley, and sat on a box and watched Charley at work. Then I thought of the dogs and went outside and found that they had been cared for. I wondered when the Commander would want to see me. All of the time the sailors and Charley and the Eskimo folks were keeping up a running fire of conversation, and I was able to gather from what they said that my dear, good friend, Professor Marvin, was indeed lost; that Peary had reached the *Roosevelt* about seven hours ahead of me; that Captain Bartlett was suffering with swollen legs and feet; that MacMillan and Borup with their own and Marvin’s boys had gone to Cape Jesup; and that Pooadloonah and Panikpah had taken their families and returned to Eskimo land.
For days after I reached the *Roosevelt*, I did nothing but rest and eat. The strain was over and I had all but collapsed, but with constant eating and sleeping, I was quickly myself again. The pains and swellings of my limbs did not come as they had on all of the other returnings, and neither was Peary troubled. Captain Bartlett was the only one of the expedition that had been out on the sea-ice who felt any after effects. Every day, a few minutes after rising, he would notice that his ankle-, knee- and hip-joints were swollen; and while the pain was not excessive, he was incapacitated for more than ten days, and he spent the most of his time in his cabin. When he came out of his cabin and did talk to me, it was only to compare notes and agree that our experiences proved that there was absolutely no question about our having discovered the Pole.

===Chinese===
理智没有离我而去，神志的缰绳也没有绷断，但那一阵子我就是歇斯底里，只晓得除了溺亡的马文，人人都平安无事。他们给我一大缸子咖啡，我喝了一勺；又递给我一杯烈酒，我一口喝干；随后有人把我搀进自己的舱房，替我脱下毛皮衣裳。六十八天来头一回，我容许自己松弛下来，沉沉睡去。
一觉醒来，摆在我面前的是想象得到的最丰盛的一席盛馔；吃完，又洗了一个再舒适不过的热水澡，然后又是一顿吃的，随后又是一觉；接着我刮了脸，梳了头，走出舱房，踱到厨房那边，坐在一只箱子上看查利干活。再后来我想起那些狗，便到外面去看，发现它们已经被照料妥当。我心里揣摩着，指挥官什么时候要召见我。这整个当口，水手们、查利和因纽特乡亲们的谈话你一言我一语，像连珠炮似的没停过；从他们的话里，我听明白了：我亲爱的好友马文教授确实遇难了；皮尔里比我早约莫七个小时抵达*罗斯福号*；巴特利特船长正吃着双腿双脚肿胀的苦头；麦克米伦和博鲁普带着他们自己和马文的队员去杰瑟普角（Cape Jesup）了；普阿德卢纳和帕尼克帕则带上家小，回因纽特人的地界去了。
回到*罗斯福号*之后的那些天，我除了休息便是吃。那场劳顿一去，我整个人几乎垮了下来，但靠着不停的吃和睡，我很快又恢复了原样。四肢的疼痛和肿胀没有像以往几回归来时那样发作，皮尔里也没有受什么罪。巴特利特船长是全队到过海冰上的人里唯一感到后遗反应的。每天起床几分钟之后，他就发觉自己的踝、膝、髋各处关节都肿了；疼痛虽不算剧烈，他却十多天行动不便，大部分时间都待在自己的舱房里。他偶或走出舱房同我说上话，也无非是相互印证见闻，一致认为我们的经历证明：我们发现了北极点，这是毫无疑义的事。

===Original===
---
Captain Bartlett, Dr. Goodsell, Chief Wardwell, Percy﻿—they could talk as they would; but the one ever-present thought in my mind was of Marvin, and of his death. I thought of him, and of his kindness to me; and the picture of his widowed mother, patiently waiting the return of her son, was before me all of the time. I thought of my own mother, whom I scarcely remembered, and I sincerely wished that it had been me who had been taken. When MacMillan and Borup returned, I learned all about the sad affair, from Kudlooktoo and Harrigan, and I feel that had he been with civilized companions the sad story of Marvin’s death would not have to be told.
On breaking camp he had gone on, leaving the boys to load up and follow him. They were going south to the land and the ship, and there was no need for him to stay with them, and when they came up to where he had disappeared, they saw the ice newly formed about him, his head and feet beneath, and nothing showing but the fur clothing of his back and shoulders. They made no effort to rescue him, and had they succeeded in getting his body out, there is little chance that they could have kept him alive, for the temperature was far below zero, and they knew nothing about restoring life to the drowned. No blame can be laid to his childish companions.
He died alone, and he passed into the great unknown alone, bravely and honorably. He is the last of Earth’s great martyrs; he is home; his work is done; he is where he longed to be; the Sailor is Home in the Sea. It is poor satisfaction to those that he left behind that his grave is the northernmost grave on the earth; but they realize that the sacrifice was not made in vain, for it was due to him that those who followed were able to keep the trail and reach the land again. The foolish boys, in accordance with Eskimo tradition, had unloaded all of Prof. Marvin’s personal effects on the ice, so that his spirit should not follow them, and they hurried on back to land and to the ship, where they told their sad story.

===Chinese===
---
